## F-165 Rename to omxcore [built on branch worktree-agent-a69d3e86978c25bd3, awaiting review; owner said go 2026-10-20; PyPI and the repo rename are the owner's]
omxcore = the core engine that apps (omxplorer, ...) build on; nobody depends on `mxframe` yet. Done in one pass at a clean point (after an M8 slice lands):
- [x] Mojo package: `mojo/mxframe/` is `mojo/omxcore/`; `scripts/build_mojo_pkg.sh` builds `build/omxcore.mojoc` and, for one stable release, the same source again as `build/mxframe.mojoc` (a .mojoc is named by its package directory, and a forwarding Mojo package would have to re-export every module and name: building twice is the plain way; about 20 s more). The package has no absolute self-imports (kernels already use `.x`); tests, tools and `mx.mojo` say `from omxcore import ...`. Proven by `tests/test_f165_rename.py`: a tiny program compiled and run against each name prints the same sum. The `mx` CLI keeps its name.
- [x] Python package: `import omxcore as mx`; `import mxframe` forwards (`compat/mxframe/__init__.py`: the same module object, and `mxframe.x` is `omxcore.x` through a meta-path alias, so `from mxframe.lazy_frame import X` and `import mxframe.native_exec as nx` give the same objects; no warning yet). The repo root is the `omxcore` package; pyproject maps `omxcore` to `.` and `mxframe` to `compat/mxframe`, so the one distribution installs both names. `pixi run test` links `omxcore` and `mxframe` (compat) into its shim; `pixi run setup-omxcore` (was `setup-mxframe`) links both into site-packages.
- [x] Text: README, docs site (nav brand, titles, descriptions, URLs), docs/*.md, STABLE.md, CONTRIBUTING, PUBLISHING, the art script and its SVGs say omxcore; URLs are `github.com/abhisheksreesaila/omxcore` and `abhisheksreesaila.github.io/omxcore/` (dead until the repo is renamed: GitHub redirects the old repo URL only after the rename). `docs/mxframe-mojo-api.md` is `docs/omxcore-mojo-api.md`; `visualize/mxframe_pipeline.html` is `omxcore_pipeline.html`. Positioning everywhere new: "a compute engine (CPU and GPU) with persistence to open Parquet files", never "a database". Old ticket text, CHANGELOG entries, release notes, briefs, design and research notes keep "MX frame" on purpose.
- [x] Tests updated (imports, paths, URLs, messages); full suites run (see the report).
- [ ] **Left as `mxframe` on purpose.** (1) The PyPI distribution name `mxframe` (pyproject `name`, `pip install mxframe`, wheel file names, `publish.yml`, the `pixi.toml` workspace and pypi-dependency name, the README badge): PyPI is the owner's call; renaming means publishing `omxcore` and making `mxframe` a one-line dependency on it. Nothing was published. (2) `MXFRAME_*` environment variables (apps pass `-D MXFRAME_JOB_THREADS` and friends): a rename needs an alias period. (3) Kernel libraries (`libmx*.so`, `kernels.mojoc`) and their hashes: `kernels_aot/*.mojo` is untouched, because editing it makes every committed library stale (the pre-commit hook) and needs a GPU rebuild. (4) Runtime paths and names: `~/.mxframe/calibration.json`, `~/.cache/mxframe/spill`, `mxframe-spill-*`, thread and MAX graph names, the test scratch directories. (5) The GitHub repo name, the issue label `mxframe` on the app's repo, the `mxframe` workspace name in `pixi.toml`, `docs/site/bench/*` data copies. (6) `MXFRAME_COMMIT` in the app's vendor folder.
- [ ] Owner, after review: rename the GitHub repo (the Pages URL follows); decide PyPI; bump STABLE to the first commit after this merge; send the mxapp issue (draft below); when the forwarding ends (one stable release later) delete `compat/`, the `mxframe` line in pyproject and the second `build_one` in `build_mojo_pkg.sh`.

**How the app (omxplorer, repo mxapp) migrates.** Nothing breaks on its own: its hourly task archives the commit named in STABLE and runs `pixi run bash scripts/build_mojo_pkg.sh`; for the current stable (23edc59) that still yields `build/mxframe.mojoc`, and for the first stable after the rename the same command yields `build/mxframe.mojoc` (old imports unchanged) next to `build/omxcore.mojoc`. To move: `cp build/omxcore.mojoc vendor/`, then `grep -rl 'from mxframe' --include='*.mojo' . | xargs sed -i 's/\bfrom mxframe\b/from omxcore/'`, and rename `vendor/mxframe.mojoc` in `scripts/mxbuild.sh`, `scripts/build.sh` and `docs/goals/*-check.py`.

Draft of the mxapp issue (not sent):
> **Title:** mxframe is now omxcore: switch your imports (the old package keeps building for one stable release)
> The engine was renamed omxcore (abhisheksreesaila/mxframe F-165). From the first stable commit after the rename, `scripts/build_mojo_pkg.sh` builds `build/omxcore.mojoc` (`from omxcore import ...`) and, for one stable release, also `build/mxframe.mojoc` from the same source, so nothing breaks if you do nothing. To switch: `cp build/omxcore.mojoc vendor/ && grep -rl 'from mxframe' --include='*.mojo' . | xargs sed -i 's/\bfrom mxframe\b/from omxcore/'`, point `scripts/mxbuild.sh`, `scripts/build.sh` and the goal checks at `vendor/omxcore.mojoc`. The env variables (`MXFRAME_JOB_THREADS`, ...), the `mx` command and the kernel libraries keep their names. After the next stable only `omxcore.mojoc` is built.

## M9 Open table formats: safe changes on plain Parquet (mimic Delta Lake) [parked: only when an app needs it]
Owner, 2026-10-19 (first principles): if omxcore ever needs to be database-like, the work mimics Delta Lake (Parquet + its transaction log): no new format, and no database work until an app needs it; the focus stays on the core engine. No file format of our own; Parquet is the storage. When an app needs incremental saves, crash safety, versions or several writers, follow the open table formats: Parquet data files + a commit log / manifest. Order to decide (read first, then write):
- [ ] read Delta Lake and Iceberg tables (the log / manifest → the live Parquet files, deletion vectors, schema evolution) as a streamed source; time travel by version
- [ ] write: append and overwrite commits, then row-level changes as small new files + a commit (a Frame's edits become a commit, not a whole-file rewrite); optimistic concurrency for several writers
- [ ] omxplorer-style apps get autosave and history from it, as a plugin over these primitives

## F-168 Window functions [done 2026-10-28] (app #17)

OVER (PARTITION BY … ORDER BY … [ROWS | RANGE frame])` in the select list and ORDER BY (`winsql.mojo` splits the statement, `window.mojo` evaluates: stable radix sort, partitions on the pool, running frames, a monotone deque for min / max): row_number, rank, dense_rank, percent_rank, cume_dist, ntile, lag / lead, first / last / nth_value, sum / count / avg / min / max over frames; with WHERE and GROUP BY; Table, Frame, Parquet and CSV scans (a scan materialises the columns), joins. ## M10 Agent-native plugins (brief `docs/briefs/positioning.md`, owner 2026-10-19) [todo, after F-169]
Plugins over generic core primitives; the core never learns about agents.
- [ ] F-170 core: a fixed-size FLOAT vector column kind (Parquet FIXED_SIZE_LIST / list<float>), and a distance + top-k kernel (cosine, dot, L2) on CPU and GPU, usable in SQL (`ORDER BY distance(v, ?) LIMIT k` with filters) and the builder; exact first, equal to a brute-force oracle

- [ ] F-172 plugin: agent search: keyword (per dictionary word) + vector + SQL filters in one call, rank fusion, results with why they matched

## M11 Linux fast path + an Omarchy edition [parked: after the core is stable with omxapp; owner 2026-10-19]
Owner: "if you want the absolute best performance, we ship Omarchy Linux with these things" (and, later, the bigger economics of shipping our own hardware with the software). The core stays portable: a Linux fast path behind build switches (as F-138b's), on automatically where the kernel supports it, with a fallback. Measure first (F-166 shows where the time goes; today streamed queries are bound by decompression on the CPU, not the disk).
- [ ] GPUDirect Storage (NVIDIA cuFile): file bytes from NVMe straight into GPU memory, skipping host RAM (the upload was 8 of every 8.75 ms per row group in F-146: the step most likely to make the GPU win on big machines too)
- [ ] io_uring with registered buffers for cold reads (many small row groups, files not in the page cache)
- [ ] huge pages for the page cache (large folios, readahead tuned), CPU pinning and NUMA placement of workers, then sched_ext experiments for barrier idle time
- [ ] build tuning: profile-guided optimisation and post-link layout, native CPU targets per release
- [ ] an Omarchy package: a tuned profile (performance governor, transparent huge pages, isolated cores), kernel flags, GPU drivers with GPUDirect Storage, so omxcore runs at full speed out of the box
- [ ] later, a business decision: a reference box (NVMe + GPU + Omarchy + omxcore) as a data appliance

## F-176 Range aggregates and block reads over a sort order [done] (mxframe #8, #9)
- [x] sum / count / mean / min / max over rows a..b of a permutation (or a row list) for a set of columns, exact at each column's scale, without per-cell calls
- [x] read rows a..b (in a permutation) of n columns in one call: typed values and null flags into caller buffers

Built (2026-10-19, branch worktree-agent-a1d41ba957acddef1): `mojo/mxframe/blocks.mojo` (new, additive; `Frame` and the executor untouched): `range_aggregate(source, [order,] cols, a, b) -> RangeResult` and
`read_block(source, [order,] cols, a, b, values, nulls)` over a `SharedTable` or a `Frame` (edits, appended and removed rows, merges, or a Frame over a Parquet file through its chunk cache); half-open ranges, an order is any row list (rows may repeat),
no order means the rows themselves. A column of the window is gathered into a dense buffer and reduced by tight loops in morsels of 16,384 positions on every core (a scan frame on the calling thread, bucketed by row group so each chunk is fetched once);
INT / DECIMAL sums exact (high and low halves per morsel, only a total outside int64 raises), the mean DuckDB's AVG bit for bit, FLOAT sums in a fixed morsel order (bit-identical with one thread and many), TEXT min / max in the case-insensitive order, nulls skipped,
NaN / -0.0 as the engine's other aggregates. Errors are plain sentences. Rules, errors and timings: `docs/mxframe-mojo-api.md` section 6b.
- Tests: `mojo/tests/test_blocks.mojo` (per-cell oracle: table, frame with 300 edits / appends / removes / a merge and again after undo, a 13-row-group scan frame, ranges empty / single / whole / across morsels and row groups, bad ranges and rows, int64 total checks, timings),
  `tests/test_mojo_range.py` (DuckDB `row_number() OVER (ORDER BY ...)` oracle through `mojo/tools/range_probe.mojo`: 4 sources x 10 orders x 10 ranges, aggregates and blocks; float sums equal between a threaded and an `-D MXFRAME_SERIAL` build), `mojo/tools/range_bench.mojo` (cost of edits). `scripts/check_mac_link.sh` OK (the probe calls both).
- Not done, on purpose: formula columns raise (`column `m` is a formula`; the work is per-row evaluation and belongs with F-179); the SQL path gives no row numbers, so an app that orders with `ORDER BY` passes the row ids of its own id column; a long range over a Frame with 300,000 edited cells pays ~120 ms to apply them.
- Review: PASS (314 adversarial runs equal DuckDB and the per-cell loop over table / scan / edited frame / edited scan frame; exact int64 boundary; FLOAT min / max / NaN as DuckDB; bits equal threaded vs serial; too-small buffers, bad ranges, rows and columns refused with writes inside the block; 8 concurrent callers). Ready for later: formula columns (serial per-row evaluation now, batched in F-179), cache the frame patch arrays by edit counter (a long range over 300k edited cells costs 80-120 ms per call), pin the reviewer's safety cases in test_blocks, a plain C record for RangeAgg

Same ratio as libzstd level 3 (4.64x), zstd save ~2x slower (0.60 s vs 0.28 s, 2M rows); checksum verified; corrupt sizes refused before allocating. Review: PASS (430k mutated inputs, no crash). ## F-187 Faster zstd saves with the Mojo codec: columns compressed in parallel, a faster matcher [done] (owner said yes 2026-10-26)
- [x] a row group's pages compressed in parallel wherever the build has threads (Linux pool; macOS / iOS with `-D MXFRAME_JOB_THREADS`, pthreads already allowed; plain Mac builds serial), byte-identical output for any thread count; `write_parquet(..., threads=n)`
- [~] single-thread zstd encoder: 0.60 s to 0.36 s on 2M rows, **1.57-1.6x libzstd level 3 (target 1.5x not met; accepted: the profile shows the encoder instruction-bound, no cheap win left)**; size within 0.2% of libzstd
- [x] the Mac link check stays green (no new symbol outside the job-threads list)
- Built: `fan.mojo` (dispatcher), columns cut into ~1 MB data pages in every build (files change on Linux too; read back by pyarrow / DuckDB, opens faster: 0.040 s to 0.022 s); all threads 0.06-0.07 s vs libzstd 0.035 s. Streamed saves were capped at `STREAM_WRITE_THREADS = 4` (memory bound; replaced by a byte budget in F-188). Review: safety clean (477 adversarial encoder inputs, no heap error; decoder unchanged; pthread fan joins and frees), must-fix was the missed 1.5x target, accepted here.

## F-188 Parallel Parquet save bounded by the memory budget [done]
- Built: `write_parquet(..., budget=)` / `Frame.save(..., budget=)`: pages start when in-flight cost (body + compressed bytes + the coder's scratch, ~12 MB per 1 MB zstd page) fits the budget, oldest always goes, written in file order by whichever thread holds the lock; bytes identical for any threads x budget; defaults 256 MB (table) and 57 MB (streamed save, four pages at real cost). `STREAM_WRITE_THREADS` removed. Streamed save: same speed and peak as F-187 (0.60 s, ~105 MB of a 110.8 MB bound; the ~101 MB floor is the decoded row group, so the margin did not grow). Budgets of 1-4 MB on a table run one page at a time (~0.35 s vs 0.06 s). Review: round 1 NEEDS WORK (scratch not counted, streamed save 50% slower), round 2 PASS.
- [ ] the pages a parallel save holds in flight are bounded by the budget (row-group bytes), not by a fixed thread cap (`STREAM_WRITE_THREADS = 4` is the workaround from F-187; the streamed-save test peaks at 105-108 MB of a 111 MB bound)
